The man sighed as he finished his piss, but before he could readjust his breeches Hart struck.  He covered the man’s mouth with one hand, and opened his throat from ear to ear with the other one.  Then, before the man could shout, he slammed the hilt of his knife as hard as he could into the man’s windpipe.  The man’s muffled scream stuttered to a stop as he could no longer get oxygen, and Hart drug him behind a tree to hide the corpse.
   He returned to the tent he had been hiding in as quickly as he could.  He didn’t have to wait long before the tents owner bent his head through the flap.  He was almost completely inside before he noticed Hart, but he was too drunk to really comprehend what he was seeing.
   Hart stabbed upward with his knife through the bottom of the man’s jaw.  It pierced his mouth and continued through his face until it exited just below his eye.  The man made a retching sound as blood poured down his throat into his lungs.  He pulled the man by his knife down onto the mat on the ground, he took the blanket and stuffed it into his mouth and nose, preventing him from talking as well as drowning him in his own blood.  Hart removed his knife from the jaw and shoved the blanket all the way to the back of the man’s throat, vomit spilled from around the edges but the majority of it went into the man’s lungs as well.  Hart thrust the knife under the man’s ribs and cursed as a mixture of vomit and blood poured from the wound.  
   Tears, puke, and blood ran down the man’s face as he shuddered and passed into eternity.  Hart wiped his blade on the man’s jerkin disgusted with the kill.  It wasn’t clean, no kill was ever clean.  The poets always spoke of the glory in death, but that was foolishness.  No death was glorious, especially not when one man kills another, when they died they choked and they stank.  Full grown men cried and begged in the face of death, when they died they soiled themselves and vomited up their meals.  No death was glorious, especially not one in battle.
   He let the man lay and peeked out through the flap of the tent, the only thing he saw was the embers of the fire dying slowly in the pit.  He crept outside, keeping his senses opened to the environment, and towards the tent where he’s seen the food being taken.  He reached the flap of the tent and opened it slowly, sliding inside without making a sound.  He turned around ready to untie the child, but came face-to-face with one of the men encamped here.  He had dark circles around his eyes, and a mucus soaked rag in his hand.
   They stared at each other for a moment, both of them to surprised to move or speak.  Than both of them acted at the same time, the man shouted just as Hart dove on him.  He silenced him by breaking his neck but it was too late, he heard the sounds of men pulling themselves off of their tents.  He ran outside, drawing his axe just in time to duck under a sword swung at his head.  He hacked at the man’s head and leapt over his body towards the fire.
   He didn’t stop when he reached it, he ran as fast as he could towards the edge of the camp throwing himself inside one of the tents on the outskirts of camp.  He landed on a body that began to make muffled grunts, he turned over and saw that it was a boy no older than sixteen tied to a large chest inside the tent. 
   Immediately he made shushing motions to the boy and he ceased making the grunting noises.  They lay silent as the sounds of men running past them towards the forest reached their ears.  Hart waited until they had been gone for thirty seconds before he bent down and cut him loose, he could see a million questions burning through his gaze but Hart shook his head, motioning again for him to be silent as he removed his gag.
   “What are you doing?”  Hart whispered as the boy began to make his way towards the tent flap.
   The boy shot him an incredulous look, “I’m going to find my weapons, I think they’re keeping them in the tent next to this one.”  
   Hart nodded and followed him out of the tent.  Luckily there were no guards left behind and they crept into the tent next to theirs.  It was larger than most of the others, and they saw why when they opened the flap.  
   There were three large chests resting between crates of valuables almost up to the ceiling of the tent.  The boy walked, not to the extravagant chests, but to a small bag leaning against the side of the tent.   Hart was perturbed as he heard the boy mutter happily for a moment, until he pulled out a crossbow from the pack.  The boy turned to him with a grin on his face and motioned towards the tent flap.
   “Ready to go then,” Hart asked.  The boy grinned and nodded to him.  Hart drew his bow and the boy laughed quietly at him, Hart raised an eyebrow at him.  They exited the tent together and made a run for the woods.  They made it about a hundred yards before they heard a shout.
   They turned to their right and Hart saw three men running towards them with their weapons drawn, he pulled his bow up and shot one of them in the chest.  Before he could nock another arrow he heard two whizzes and the other two fell to the ground with bolts protruding from their chests.  
   Saving his questions for later, Hart and the boy ran back to where Sarai lay hidden in the brush.  Motioning to both of them to keep quiet they continued down the road for three miles before setting up their mats in a patch of brush fifty feet from the road.
   Better be some kid, Hart thought just before he fell asleep.




Chapter 31:  Unwarranted Attention


   
   It was ten days before Ignis and Droft made it to Shan.  The journey had been easy, the road offered up no challenges to them that they had difficulty overcoming.  In Wystland there had been a little trouble regarding a drunken Droft and another, possibly more drunken, soldier who had been insulted by his comments regarding the legions of Trojia.  They had had to leave that night, but other than a severely blackened eye the only thing injured about him had been his pride.
   Luckily they had already bought supplies, and they made it the rest of the way to Shan in about four days.   There were two inns in the town, one was obviously tailored to the soldiers and workers.  They chose this one and waited for the three or four days it would take for Sarai and Revlin to arrive.
   Hart, Revlin, and Sarai made their way to Shan two or three days later and, consequently, chose to stay at the same inn.  Ignis hadn’t even had to look for them, the girl walked up behind him and sat next to him at the bar.  He looked over at her and recognized her immediately, obviously she had recognized him first because she had chosen to sit next to him.  He tapped Droft on the shoulder and motioned towards her, Hart and Revlin had joined her now, and a look of shock appeared on his face.  He blinked a few times then followed them over to a corner of the Tavern, where they would be less likely to be overheard.
   Once they’d settled into their seats Droft whispered to Ignis, the smell of alcohol was heavy on his breath.  “I thought you said there was only two of ‘em?”  Ignis shrugged.
   “I was just wonderin’ abou’ that meself actually, who is this with you?”  Revlin and Sarai both looked at the man sitting in between them, he was strong and built like a soldier.  He wore no insignia on his jerkin so Ignis assumed he was a hired sword.  
   “The name’s Hart Treeman, I’m a sellsword from Remes.  I was on my way to Wystland, some offer about a caravan guard, when I came upon this one.”  He gestured at Sarai and continued, “Some bandits had captured her companion and she was going to try and rescue him herself.”  His jaw set squarely as he clenched his teeth.  “See I couldn’t let those bandits have their way with her, you all know their ways with women.”
   Droft shook his head, “Horrible, bloody horrible.”  They were silent for few moments until Hart continued.
   “So I helped her rescue the boy, and we made our way here.”  He nodded to them, worry lines appeared on his face like he was about to say something but decided better, for when Sarai spoke he listened intently.
   “Hart I don’t know what we would’ve done without you, ask of us anything and I promise we shall do our best to repay you.”  Her eyes seemed to sparkle when Hart looked into them, he didn’t even stop to think before he stated his response.
   “Take me with you.”  Ignis bit his lip and looked around at Revlin and Sarai.  Something unspoken passed between them because, after a few seconds, Ignis shrugged and resumed drinking his ale.
   Sarai looked up at him happily, “It would be a pleasure if you would accompany us the rest of the way to Ford.  We really could use all the help we can get.”  Revlin nodded and spoke, perhaps for the first time at the table.
   “Hart I owe you my life, I believe that if you came with us there would be plenty of opportunities for me to repay you along the way.  I…do not wish to continue on while in the debt of another man.  It is a power that has broken thrones, and I will pay you back any way possible.”  Droft had begun crying into his mead, the tears spilling over his face.
   “Bloody people, a man saved me life once.  He-e was killed the next day, an’ I never paid ‘im back.”  He sobbed harder into his hands.  “Drata be his name, won’t never forget it.”  He looked up from his hands at the rest of them seated around the table.  “That’s why I’m ‘ere, you people are the las’ ‘ope we’ve got.”  Revlin shot a warning glance at Ignis and he began to try and calm him down, but it seemed like he wasn’t planning to stop anytime soon.  “Descendants of the Great Stewards, and bloody three of ‘em right ‘ere in front of me, somethin’s wrong with this world.  Powers be a stirrin’, but I’ll be damned afore I let any one of ‘em hurt any of yeh.”  
   He was wailing now, half the bar had turned around when they heard what he was saying, half of them with looks of interest and hope on their faces.  Others had looks of fear, and even in some others, anger.  
   One man who had been sitting in the corner stood up, he straightened his hooded cloak against his body and began to walk towards them slowly.  Ignis was trying to comfort him and so didn’t notice when he pulled a knife out of his coat.  Revlin shouted in alarm, but the knife was already in the air headed towards Ignis’s neck.
   Sarai screamed and air around Ignis seemed to have become solid, because the knife sunk into it like it would’ve done a stump.  A resounding crack filled the air and Sarai fell to the table, unconscious.  The man drew two more blades from his belt and ran towards them, with a red glint in his eye.
   At once all three men stood up from the table, while Revlin sank below the table and was going through his bags in earnest.  Ignis picked up the knife from the table and drew his own saxe from his belt, Hart unslung his axe, and Droft drew the long Chromatis blade from its sheath on his back.
   The assassin, for that was surely what he was, advanced towards them with lighting speed.  Hart barely had time to duck as one of those long knives whirled over his head.  Ignis saw this and took advantage of the opening by hurling one of his knives at the man.  His accuracy and strength were such that no man could have evaded the blade, somehow though the blade missed his heart and hit him in the shoulder.  The man howled as he jerked the blade out of his shoulder and sent it flying towards its owner.
   Ignis managed to deflect the blade with his saxe, and ran forward to help Droft and Hart, they were having a difficult time just staving him off.  He joined in the dance, the three men flowed together like it had been rehearsed.  One seemed to know where another would strike before the blow landed, and likewise so that the three men were more like one man with six arms.
   Somehow the assassin was still fighting, he had taken several injuries but they did not seem to bleed like they would on a normal man.  He threw back his hood and spat blood, hot and sticky, onto Hart’s face causing him to shout out and try to wipe the stinging liquid from his eyes.  Then the assassin was gaining ground, he managed to nick Ignis in the ribs, then punch Droft in the chin.  The two still fought, but it began to seem more and more like a lost cause.  Hart managed to hack at the man’s legs with his axe, but was kicked in the chest with such force that he flew ten feet and landed on a table, knocked cold.
   Finally Revlin had found what he had been looking for, the flame arc was in his hands and he shouted for the two to duck.  Just in time flames jumped through the air and stuck to the assassin, he began to writhe and scream as the flames ate away at his flesh.  After what seemed like hours, or maybe just seconds, the man collapsed to the floor, sounds of crackling flesh and smells of burnt hair filled the now empty inn.
   One man it seemed had hidden behind the bar, he was nearly bald and he rose from his hiding place warily.  “Wha-what the bloody ‘ell was tha about?”  He shouted across the common room.  The six looked at each other, wondering what they should tell the man, but before they made up their minds he was shouting again.  
   “I don’t want no blazin’ runaways in my inn, you bloody scared away every las’ one o me guests.  I wan’ every las’ one of you out.”  He looked at them with a crazed look in his eye, when none of them moved he pointed at the door and shouted his final warning.  “I bloody mean it, you better leave now, or I’ll call the guard!”  
   They all looked at each other and the innkeeper saw one of them shrug.  Then they gathered their belongings from under the table, and walked out the door without even a word of apology or thanks.
   Hmmph, the bartender thought.  Look at what they did to my inn!  He looked around at the broken chairs, and upturned tables.  I don’t  want involved, I’ll fix the place up and won’t bloody mention it again.  
   Without another word he walked over to the corner, grabbed a broom, and began to clean up what was left of the ravaged inn.




Chapter 32: Reptilian Acquaintances


   “Here take a look at it yourself!”  Lief threw a blackened acorn at him.  He reached up and caught it with his free hand.  It looked like someone had tried to roast it, only they’d left a whole pile of them like it.  
   “Whadya think?  Doesn’t seem very likely that a Y’marr did this, they’re all bloodletters here.”  He tossed the acorn to Brazen, who closed his eyes and tightened his palm around the nut.
   After about thirty seconds his eyes opened and his eyebrow wrinkled in thought.  “The energies have been worked inside this nut, similar to when Eric uses his gift.”  He shook his head, “I don’t know maybe the last person that came through these parts was a crafter who wasted time by the campfire roasting nuts.”
   Adelia shrugged, “I think that it matters very little.  He is not hampering us.  We should be on our way, we’re only two days out of Thumbria.”  
   Brazen nodded his head, “Yes my dear I agree…” he turned towards the other two, who had been passing the nut back and forth, “C’mon you two, we’re heading out.”  They stopped passing the nut and threw it into the woods, they heard it as it passed through the brush and then heard a yelp of pain.  They all turned towards where they’d heard the sound.  
   There was a scampering sound and Eric saw a flash of blue sprint towards the deeper part of the woods.  He leapt of his horse, ran past his wyvern who was following behind them, and sprinted off the road.
   He heard their shouts behind him but he ignored them, he drew from his source and doubled his speed.  Using his sensitivity he followed the heat trail left by the person in front of him.  He sped through the woods, narrowly missing trees and stumps in his way.
   As he came over the next ridge he stopped abruptly, he saw a large camp splayed out along the valley.  He hadn’t heard of any bandits in this area, but wait these tents were made out of animal skins not canvas like normal ones.
   Then he saw movement in the cluster of tents, people were doing chores like they did in the city.  Children ran through the dirt paths that wound in and out among the tents, dodging between mothers carrying laundry to and from the creek that ran along the western edge of the camp.  Men were carrying animals over their shoulders that looked like deer as they came and went from the woods around them.
   He was just about to turn away from the camp splayed out before him when he heard a shout from behind him.
   “Stop outlander, no do not turn.”  He had nearly turned towards the voice, but he’d caught a glimpse of more than one man, all of them were holding spears pointed towards him.
   “Alright I’m not going to turn, what do you want from me?”  When they didn’t answer anger shot through him.  “If you’re not going to do anything to me than let me go, what use is it to hold me here like this?” 
   One of the men snorted and spoke in a strange language, strangely though Eric found that he could understand them.  
   “Le tua de vette?  Bruin valle trevard!”  Hear how he speaks?  He would destroy himself!  The men around him began to laugh, but Eric turned sharply towards them.  He shot energy into his palms so that they crackled like lightning.
   “Destroy myself?  Ha, I think you’ve fallen a bit over your heads my friends.”  The men were stunned, then one of them dropped his spear and walked towards him warily, his palms facing Eric to show he meant no harm.  He lowered his voice so that only Eric could hear.
   “Vette Trajettes?”  You speak the Tongue?  Eric frowned.
   “Of course I speak the tongue, I’ve spoken Common all my life.”  The man shook his head.
   “En en, undetre Trajettes.”  No no, THIS tongue.  He started to shake his head but he’d noticed a tiny trickle of energy had covered the man’s tongue when he spoke to him like that.  No other person had ever invested  energy into their mouths when they spoke before.  Maybe he was just becoming more aware of regular body functions.
   “I’m afraid I don’t know what you mean, I can understand you perfectly well.  I don’t hear any difference in the way you spoke earlier.”  The man scrunched up his brow and bit his lip for a moment, then spoke again carefully.
   “De grandjette Alir?”  Do you work the great Alir?  Eric frowned.
   ”Alir?  I don’t understand, what do you mean?”  The man started to speak, but frowned and returned to speaking Common.
   “Are you a crafter?”  He nodded warily.
   “Yes I’m a crafter.  I’d like to know why that makes any difference whether or not I can speak a different language though.”  The man shook his head.
   “No no, you don’t understand yet…but you will.  Will you come with us?”  
   He realized he hadn’t let go of the energy filling his palms, he cut off the flow and nodded.
   “Good, I wish for you to speak with the challa she will know of your gift.”  He had begun to walk towards the village, but stopped.
   “Whadya mean my gift?  I don’t understand why I need to go and see this person.  Why can’t I just leave now?”
   The man shrugged.  “You are free to leave whenever you wish, we are not barbarians who would keep you against your will.  But know this, if challa thinks you have the gift then she will teach you to work the Alir.  She is the keeper of our secrets, but her knowledge is not shared lightly.  She has only taught one outlander in her lifetime, and challa live a very long time.”  
   Eric frowned, then nodded.  He followed the men down the slope towards the camp below.  When they reached what looked like a line of ground charcoal in the ground the men before him changed before his eyes.  What had looked like regular, if not slightly more muscled, men before now looked like large, lightning blue, reptilian creatures.
   He gasped, it wasn’t just the men that had changed.  The entire village was full of creatures like the men in front of him.
   The leader turned when he realized Eric had stopped, his mouth crumpled up at the edges in what he took as a grin.  “Do we startle you in this form outlander?  Fear not, you are in no danger here.”  He focused on Eric his eyes widening, then he shook his head.  “Yes, challa will want to speak with you.  Quickly now, let us make haste.”
   The creatures started into a trot towards the large tent in the center of the village.  Eric quickly tried to keep pace with them.  Their long legs made it hard, but if he jogged he could keep up with them fairly easily.
   When they arrived at the tent the leader held up his hand.  “Wait here outlander, I will speak to challa and inform her that you are here.  She has been waiting for you I think.”  Before Eric could respond the creature slipped into the tent soundlessly.
   He had barely waited for twenty seconds before the leader was back.  He motioned for Eric to come inside.  Eric stepped through the tent flap and into the spacious interior.  
   The walls were lined with many quilts and vases, there were animal pelts covering the chairs and the table.  Everything about the tent seemed to be finely crafted, strangely though there was no bed, only a pile of straw in one corner of the room that looked particularly lived in.  He was still admiring the room when he heard a small voice coming from his left.
   “Se...vette de Trajette?”  So…you speak the Tongue?  Eric was frustrated, of course he bloody well could understand the flaming tongue.
   “Yes…I speak the tongue.  Although nobody around here has taken the time out to explain to me what the ‘tongue’ is.”  He glared at the woman, if she could be called that.
   He had heard the other people call the challa a her, so he assumed she was.  Otherwise she looked like a smaller, and frailer version of exactly what he’d seen outside the tent.  Strangely though instead of green her eyes glowed purple.
   She chuckled at him.  “This is a surprise indeed, not only has he a Gifted relar he has a gifted tongue as well.”  
   He frowned at her.  “Alright here’s the deal, I’ll sit down and listen to what you have to say for no more than twenty minutes.  After that I’m afraid I’ll have to be on my way.  My friends are waiting for me back on the road.”
   The woman’s eyes narrowed.  She stared at him for a moment and then let loose a long hiss.  He tried not to show it, but it had rightly terrified him.  “I must admit it has been awhile since I have been spoken to in such a manner.  As a matter of fact the last time was when dear Gervard was here last.  But I suppose we could speak as equals, if you are proven worthy.”  
   That name…hadn’t he heard it somewhere before?  It was in one of his history studies.  He’d been, one of the kings maybe.  He thought for a few seconds before his mouth dropped.  
   “You trained the second king of Trojia?”  The thing grinned.
   “I suppose that’s what you would call him, he was no great man.  The son of Trojja was not nearly as powerful as his father.”  The woman shivered.  “I have never been truly scared before I met that man, I admit in the face of that much power I was naught but a leaf in the whirlwind.  Powerless to be tossed wherever he wished.”  
   Eric was silent, how old was this woman?  She claimed to have known Trojja himself, that means she must be at least nine-hundred years old, if not more.
   “But alas, we are not here to speak of them.  We have more important matters to attend to.”  She reached up to scratch her skin.   “Yes child, I would first have to test you.  It is nothing at all really, merely to test your potential.  You are a crafter yes?”
   Eric nodded, “Yes I am.”
   “Then that will make this much easier, all I ask you to do is to invest as much energy as possible into me.  Send me flying around this tent like a rag doll.”  She smiled at him expectantly.  “You may start when you wish.”
   Eric frowned.  “I don’t think that would be a good idea.”
   The woman hissed.  “It would also not be a good idea to disappoint me, I insist.”
   Eric shrugged, he sent a trickle of power into the woman, enough to knock her over.  He sent it from his own body, not his source, so he felt a small amount of weakness from the attempt.
   Strangely the woman hadn’t even twitched, she glance at him expectantly looking smug.
   He’d felt his energy being diverted inside of her, like gravity pulling the energy towards her.  He frowned and sent enough energy towards her that she should have been knocked into the edges of the tent.  Again she didn’t even twitch.
   With a snarl he tapped into the source, her glum look changed to a wide-eyed grin.  His anger focused it onto her as he sent a spiral of mechanical energy straight into her sternum.  Her jaw locked, and she slid backwards a few feet like she was being pushed but she still hadn’t fallen over.
   Eric grinned, he hadn’t even focused a third of his power yet.  He raised his hands and doubled the amount of force being exerted, he saw the woman topple then fall to the ground.  She slid on her back all the way into the edge of the tent, but still managed to block the most of the stream.
   He stopped the flow and walked over to her, offering his arm to her.  She ignored it and stood up without his help.  She walked over to the table and sat down stiffly.  Then, surprisingly, she raised her hands and began to clap.
   Perturbed he took the seat opposite of her and waited for her to speak.  She seemed to be wondering what to say, but after a moment she spoke to him with her head looking down at the table.
   “My name is Sa’shaset, young child.  I am willing to take you under my tutelage for the next two months.  During these two months you will learn to control the Alir and focus the relar.  In time you may be one of the best Drunair I have ever seen, if you are willing to put forth the effort.”  She glance up at him and sighed.
   “Wait a minute, why are you offering this to me?  And what makes you think that I would choose to learn from you anyway?”  The woman’s head shot up and a low hiss escaped from her throat.
   “Simply because we have knowledge no one else does.  The Drunair are the most skilled workers of the Alir one the face of Mercolas.”  She grinned.  “Your people would say that we can turn rocks into gold, and coal into jewels.  They would say that we can heal any wound, find wells underground, and that we have the strength of one-hundred crafters.”  She looked at Eric’s dropped jaw and laughed aloud. 
   “Your people would call us Fringe Banes.”




























Chapter 33: Deep Corruption


   Sparks leapt from his skin as hammer blows rained down upon it.  They no longer dented his flesh, he no longer felt any pain.  His flesh had become iron, and his will had become steel.  
   He glanced down at his hand, the fingers had become claws, tips that could cut through any being.  He raked them along the walls of his stone prison and laughed as sparks erupted from where he scratched it.  This was power.
   He’d had a name once, it floated there at the edge of his consciousness.  What had they called him those years ago.
   Sven, yes that had been his name.  It no longer was.  He was no longer that boy from the village, nor was he longer anything that could resemble a human being.  At first he’d clung to his name, it was the only thing that had held his sanity together for the first fifteen years or so.
   He had gone mad after that, his sanity had been lost to him for nearly eight hundred years of torture.  Those years were lost to his memory, he had no recollection of them.  
   Then he went sane, the pieces inside him that had snapped had been welded back together.  His broken mind had been remade in that pit, and it was more glorious then it had ever been.
   Today was the last day, it had been nine-hundred and ninety-nine years, eleven months, and thirty days.  Tonight at midnight he would join the other potentials in the court of the beast.  He would enter his dark army and assemble with the other Gha’rhed there.
   He stewed for the rest of the day, ignoring even the worst torture the lava bath, and waited until he was thrown in the pressure chamber.  Once he was crushed by all sides in that stone cave he would finally be initiated.
   He felt the rain of lava pouring on his face stop, and he was pulled from the shackles by three of the ra’vehn, they were the slaves of Oblivion.  He grimaced at their eyeless faces as they drug him to the hole in the ground that entered the pressure chamber.
   Happiness flooded through him as they threw him into the hole.  He curled into a ball and cried tears of happiness as the walls broke his bone beneath the weight of thousands of tons of stone.  He ignored the pain, he knew he would be healed when it was over, and caressed the walls.  Gone would be the days where he would do nothing and sit being cared for, now were the days of his knighthood to the Beast.
   It stopped after a few hours, and he was pulled from the hole by long hooks held by ra’vehn.  He lay on the ground, broken, as the medica poured dark energy into his veins.  Every time he was broken and healed in this manner he became stronger.  The darkness became a larger part of him every time he was healed.  Now there was no shred of humanity left in him.
   As he stood he noticed an armored gha’rhed standing in the corner of the room.  This man had shaped differently than he had.  His fists had become great mauls, and his entire body was covered by the armored growth so that only his burning red eyes were visible beneath it.  He stood and approached the gha’rhed in the glow of the molten light.
   When he was ten feet from the man he stopped.  The gha’rhed looked him over as he would a piece of merchandise, lifting his arm to test his armor, watching him attack a rock dummy with precision, and observing everything he did for the last hour of his freedom.  Satisfied with the results the gha’rhed motioned for him to follow him out of the torture chambers.
   He followed the gha’rhed out of the chambers and into the sulphur fields.  The Tower of Transmodim loomed over them as they approached it.  His feet nearly slipped more than once on the burning earth, but they made it the rest of the way to the tower quickly.
   They entered its walls and the initiate admired the great onyx fixtures lining the hallways.  Every hall ended with an arch of red energy blocking the way through.  At every arch the gha’rhed raised his hand to let them through.
   He followed the gha’rhed for another minute before chancing a question.  He asked it in the thick guttural tongue that was spoken in Oblivion.  “Where are we going now?”  His voice sounded like two stones grinding together, and he coughed after saying it.
   “You are going to the reshnak dividai, to prove your worth to the Great One.”  The ghe’rhed’s voice was lower, like a boom of thunder.
   He said nothing as they went higher and higher into the tower, passing great halls of feasting, and the chambers of the damned.  Finally they arrived at two great doors that opened to the entryway for the Great One’s chambers.
   The gha’rhed motioned for him to enter and held his hand to the door.  It opened and the initiate passed through it.  He walked slowly through the throne room of the Great One.  Shadow covered the entire chamber, so he walked slowly through the pitch black room.  
   He continued to walk forward until he heard a low chuckle vibrate through the air.  
   Relax, you are not in danger here dark child
   He visibly relaxed his posture and peered around the chamber, anxious to see the Great One’s physical form.  He chanced a question.  “Great One, my life is yours.  I am here to seek the reshnak dividai, to prove my worth to you.  How may I find the trial?”  Immediately a voice boomed through the chamber.
   INSOLENT WORM!  DID YOU THINK I WAS IGNORANT, THAT I HAD NOT INVITED YOU HERE TO BEGIN WITH?  I KNOW YOUR EVERY THOUGHT, EVERYWHERE THERE IS DARKNESS OR SHADOW I AM.  EVEN IN YOUR OWN HEART!
   He fell to his knees at the attack on his senses.  Just the presence of the Great One’s anger nearly killed him.  It was worse pain than any of his tortures had been.  He prostrated himself and begged for forgiveness.  The Beast’s mood changed just as quickly as it had before.
   If you seek the trial it lies behind my throne, beware.  If you seek the path of the gha’rhed therein lies the most danger.  The voice paused for a moment, then continued.  But if you seek an even higher honor, my servant, you may pass through the path of the Y’marr, I am in need of a new presence in Mercolas.  U’mar has failed me.
   His heart leapt.  “I would seek the path of the Y’marr Great One, if it is pleasing to you.”  He felt emotion roiling through the shadow, then a glowing hole was rent into the air.  He could not see through it, but he knew this was where the Beast required him most.  He approached it slowly and reached out with his hand through the hole.
   Immediately he was pulled inside of it.  Pressure crushed every inch of his body as he was pulled through the fabric of time and space.  It was over just as fast as it had happened, and he opened his eyes in a large chamber surrounded by thousands of people.
   Only they were not people.  They were the servants of the Beast, all different shapes of them had crowded into the arena.  Across from where he stood there was another man.  He was chained, but his eyes burned with the power of the Shadow.  His hair was blood red and his skin had become covered with the blood that had run through his veins.
   A bloodletter he thought.  They were the only servants that bled.  They were also the only servants that could partake in sexual intercourse, or food or drink of any sort.  Because of this only the most loyal were permitted to join them.
   A voice shouted from above them.  THE TRIAL OF THE Y’MARR HAS BEGUN.  MY SERVANT WILL FACE THE TRAITOR U’MAR IN COMBAT TO THE DEATH FOR THE RIGHT OF THE POSITION OF Y’MARR.  IF HE SUCCEEDS, THEN HE WILL TAKE HIS POSITION AMONG THE SIX.  IF HE FAILS, I WILL LET U’MAR HAVE HIM.
   A roar ran through the crowd as he ran his claws over the walls.  He ran for U’mar just as his chains were released.
   They met in the center of the arena, U’mar lacked his piece of the heart so he was nowhere near as powerful as he had been.  The initiate brought his claws down upon his knee almost immediately, and U’mar leapt back in shock and stared at the blood pouring down his thigh.  His eyes widened as the initiate closed in on him.
   U’mar fled along the edge of the arena, begging for mercy from the crowd.  The initiate walked towards him slowly.
   “Please stop, I-I will share power with you.  If you let me live, I will be your most loyal partner.  We could share the heart, I beg you.”  The initiate laughed in his face.
   “I would no sooner share it with an insect.  You are mine worm.”  
   The man’s face contorted with rage and he leapt at the initiate in a final desperate lunge.  His hands closed on his face and his nails dug into his eyes.  He plunged his claws into the man’s back and felt pleasure for the first time in a long while as they buried themselves to the palm in his flesh.
   He grunted and lifted the body into the air.  He held it high for the crowd to see then rent it in two with a powerful jerk of his arms.  The man screamed as he tore at his intestines, and strung them out along the ground.  He tore the Y’marr to bloody pieces as the crowd cheered from their seats.  
   When he was finally dead the initiate stood and raised his hands to the crowd.  Again that loud booming voice rang through the arena.
   U’MAR HAS FAILED TO RECLAIM HIS TITLE.  THIS INITIATE PASSES THE TEST.  ACCEPT THE NEW Y’MARR OF THE THIRD SEPT.  HIS NAME SHALL BE ZRADIST, MAY ALL WHO HEAR THE NAME FEAR ITS POWER, AND QUIAL BENEATH HIS TERROR.
   Zradist raised his arms high as the crowd cheered his name.  
Chapter 34:   A Decision Made


   Eric found his tongue after a moment.  
   “Sa’shaset, I am afraid I cannot accept your offer.”  She made a gesture that seemed the equivalent to biting her lip, something between a wrinkled nose and risen eyebrows.  She opened her mouth, paused, and then closed it again.  Her talons rapped the table idly as she stared into his eyes.  Hers were shimmering blue, not like the normal blue of a human’s eye, but more like a living color.  They were deep and full of intelligence.
   ”You do realize what you are refusing Trojian?”  Her nails clacked against the table a little faster.  “Without the knowledge of the Alir you would just as likely destroy yourself as the Y’marr.”  At his wide-eyed look the ends of her jaw twitched.  “Yes I know of your quest, a chosen life of reclusiveness does not mean ignorance of the world.”  She paused, then shrugged.  “It just means you have to listen a little closer.”
   Eric bit his lip, would it be worth it to learn from the Fringe Banes?  Could their knowledge of Alir really aid him in destroying the Y’marr?  
   “I…I must think on this, and consult with my companions.  If I stayed, how long would it take for me to become a-“he made a motion with his hands to the Chala.
   “A Drunair.”  He nodded.  “Typically those who wish to join the ranks of the Drunair begin their training planning to spend five years before becoming accepted.”  Her tail twitched.  “But for you…I am unsure.  Maybe five years, maybe five months, the only one who determines how long the apprenticeship lasts is you.”
   Truth be told he wasn’t surprised, if he accepted tutelage from Sa’shaset and learned to control energy the way she could he would be mighty indeed.  
   “Like I said, I will consult my companions.  We will come to a decision together.  I am somewhat…crucial to our goals.”  He grimaced.  “I just do not want to make a hasty decision that may lead to distasteful consequences later down the road.”  
   She smiled knowingly.  “Go, you will have a place in our little village for the night.  Rest and plan.  I will hear your decision in the morning.”  She stretched her arms.  “I am in need of a rest anyway, it has been awhile since I have practiced the Stone by the River.”  She picked a piece of something from her teeth and flicked it onto the ground.  “It has left me fatigued.”  And without another word she shooed him out of her tent and closed the flap behind him.
   Unsure of where to go he began to walk back the way he had come before.  He was met by the same hunter who had taken him to the Chala.  “Hello young blood, has the Chala agreed to teach you?”  Eric looked into his eyes, he did not seem anything more than mildly interested on the outside, but inside he could tell that the hunter was anxious to hear his answer.
   “The Chala has agreed to teach me.”  Eric said softly.
   “You will have a difficult few years, but I am sure she sees great potential in you.  It is a good thing to be offered the secrets of the Drunair.”  He turned and looked Eric in the eyes for the first time.  His eyes were startlingly blue, like the color of a deep lake or a piece of sapphire.  His brow sharpened to a v-shape.  He looked as if he had a question to ask, but wasn’t sure how to ask it.
   Before Eric could say anything they were at the edge of the village and he could just hear the shouts from his companions over the ridge.  Adelia and Lief both came sprinting through the bushes, weapons drawn.
   When they saw him standing next to the fringe bane a startled look came over their faces.  Lief turned to Eric, “We-we thought something was the matter.”  He shifted his gaze quizzically towards the fringe bane for half a second, then back to Eric.  “Who is this you’re with?”
   A deep rumbling came from the fringe bane beside him, it took a step forward and lowered its head to stare into his eyes.  “You mean what is this you are with.”  Then he rose up to his full height.
   “Y-yes I suppose that was what I meant.”  His hand twitched towards his knives concealed in his sleeve.  Eric stepped forward.
   “Lief it’s alright he is a friend.  This is where the fringe banes have taken up.  They are settling here for a season.”
   Now a look of surprise came over both of their faces.  “What do they want of you?”  Adelia asked.  
   Eric glanced nervously up to the chieftan beside him.  “I-I think they would like me to stay here for awhile.  Their leader has offered to train me to be a Drunair.”  
   She frowned, obviously confused by the word.  Lief, however, widened his eyes to the point that his eyebrows almost disappeared beneath his hair.  “Truly, they will do this for you?”  Eric nodded and looked up nervously at the chieftan beside him.
   “This is not normal for my people to share their ways with an outsider.  We are fierce to guard our secrets.  But for this one the Chala has made a special exception.”  His eyes flicked towards Eric, and his tongue flicked out of his mouth.  “She must see much potential to agree to such a thing.”  
   Silence settled over the four of them.  After a moment the chieftan shook his shoulders and began to walk away.  He had taken about five steps before he looked over his shoulder at them.  “If you follow this path you will get back to the road in about a quarter hour.  You may bring whatever belongings you have with you to our village.  Ask for Sinque, she will show you where to sleep.”  
   Before any of them could offer up thanks he walked off towards the center of the community.  The three looked at each other, then followed the path he’d shown them back to the road.
   They arrived shortly, and were barraged by a series of descriptive questions by Brazen.  After five or ten minutes of this, and after they had told him all that had happened, he exhaled deeply with a sigh.  “For you to do this would be to abandon our quest for at least a year.  I don’t believe that we have that kind of time on our hands.  Even now forces are moving, battle lines are being drawn.  I just don’t think there’s any time for you to learn to be a Drunair.  If you were one then I would have no fear that you could kill the Y’marr, but we simply do not have the time.”  He shook his head, and rubbed at his eyelids.
   Eric stood straight, he knew this.  He’d been so excited that he had forgotten the very reason why he needed the training.  He’d have to go back and tell the Chala why he couldn’t stay, how would they be able to work the energy into the Chromatis without him anyway?  Yes he would have to tell her, there was no way they could wait another year.
   He walked back to the fringe bane camp.  The whole way there solutions kept creeping up onto his mind, he wanted this power.  To become a Drunair would mean that no man could stand against him and his wishes.  But he had other obligations, maybe after they had destroyed the Y’marr he could come back and train with her.
   He walked straight to the Chala’s tent and opened the flaps.  He walked in and saw that she was sitting at her table, writing something.  She raised her eyes to him as he walked towards her, then lowered her quill and crossed her hands together.  Waiting for him to speak.
   He bowed stiffly, then began to relay to her everything that Brazen had told them back at the road.  He told her about their plan, and why there was no way he could wait even as long as a year before finishing their task.  Through the entire conversation she said nothing, simply nodded and motioned for him to continue to talk.  
   As soon as he finished she raised a hand.  She looked down at her stack of papers, and then back up at him.  She swallowed, and opened her mouth slowly, as if she was trying to decide exactly what to say.  
   “You are going to decline to becoming a Drunair?  I am simply making sure that I heard you correctly.  You are going to leave this camp and do what you just told me about?”  Eric nodded, with a new resolve edging around his features.
   “Yes I am.  I had already agreed to this, and I am a crucial part of the task at hand.  Without me there is no chance of success.”  
   The Chala nodded.  She stood up and walked over to the dresser stand that sat beside of her bed.  She pulled a sack out from under her bed, and tipped all of the drawer’s contents into it.  Confused, Eric opened his mouth to ask her what she was doing.  She silenced him with a look, and continued to gather up the rest of her things.
   She walked towards the tent flap and motioned for him to follow.  They walked through the camp until they came to an area where all the tents were of equal size, and were ordered into columns.  It was most likely where the hunters, or warriors resided.  
   Chala walked through the gaps between the tents and made her way to the tent that was situated in the middle of the others.  It was slightly larger, but would still only allow room for a person to barely stand inside of it.  Chala whistled and the chieftan came out of the tent, followed by two other hunters.  He looked down at her bags in surprise.
   “I will be leaving, Sakra, for a while.  My student is travelling and I will be going with him.  My help may be crucial to the success of his journey.”  The chieftan didn’t say anything, just stared at her in shock.  “I do not know how long I will be gone, most likely it will be for a year or more.  I would like you to take command of the people while I am gone.  I trust that you will follow the directions I have laid out on my desk for you.”  
   Before Sakra could respond, the Chala turned and walked away.  Eric followed her, looking back at the now disheveled chieftan.  When they reached the edge of the clearing she stopped.  She motioned for Eric to lead the way from here, and they began the walk back to the road.
   They arrived there after fifteen minutes of walking, and Chala walked right up to their wagon and deposited her belongings into it.  Then she jumped inside and curled up next to a crate of food and yawned.  “Lessons start tomorrow, do not wake me.  I need to be fresh by then so I can show you the Alir.”  With that she turned away from him and began to sleep.
   He turned, and walked over to where his wyvern lay in the grass.  It was a warm day now, and he had yet to come up with a good name for it.  He woke it up and ran a few more names by it like he had before, none of them stuck so he contented himself with dozing on its back for another twenty minutes.  Then he patted it on the head and told it to get ready to move.  They could hopefully get five or six miles further by the end of the day.  
   And things are looking up now, everything’s set into motion.  Now we just wait and see how the cards play out.  He walked beside his wyvern, playfully fingering its straps as he did.


























Chapter 35:   Coming Together


   Chala’s lessons turned out to be more peculiar then he expected.  Some days they would sit in the wagon listening for “the silence.”  Others she would simply tell him to carry her for the entire day.  The days blurred together, and they passed through the town of Yirra after four days of insistent riding.  
   Three more days of this passed, and finally she began to teach him of the Alir.
   “Melar, when you work the Alir it is like trying to hold water in your hand.  Sometimes it is impossible no?”  He nodded.  Melar meant student in her tongue.  “How would you hold water in your hand Melar?”  
   He just shook his head.  “I do not know Chala, I have never tried.”  She snorted.
   “And yet you have worked the Alir, this is interesting is it not?”  He shrugged, and settled down to listen.  
   She reached into her pack, and cursed as the wagon hit a bump in the road.  She pulled out a round object that had a small piece of metal inside of it.  It looked like it was spun out of glass, but that the maker had inserted this fragment inside on purpose.  She handed it to him.
   He held the object in his hands and turned it to look at it from different angles.  It seemed like the type of thing rich people kept on their dressers.  Little trinkets and such.  He handed it back to her after a moment, and she held it up so he could see.
   “Do you know what this is?”  He shook his head.  “This is a lightstone, if a crafter or Drunair is skilled enough he can make it shine brighter than a fire.”  He cocked his head to the side.
   “Can you show me Chala?”  This was standard for their lessons, he had learned that she would not continue sometimes unless he asked.
   She nodded and closed her eyes, he felt the normal chill enter the air that happened whenever she drew energy from her surroundings.  Only a few seconds later a web of lights appeared on the lightstone.  Then they spread, and grew brighter until it shone so bright that he had to turn his head.
   “Are you studying the Alir?  I will only hold this for another thirty seconds, and I will expect you to duplicate it.”  Flinching from her words he focused on the weaves of Alir flowing through the glass.  They were unlike normal strands, each one was made out of three braided together.  He could tell from the Chala’s strain that this was not a simple weave.
   She let it go with a sigh and the light faded from the lightstone slowly.  She handed it to him, “When you let it go do not do it suddenly.  You must let the energy drain away slowly, not all at once.  If you do then you will break it, and that is my only lightstone.”  Her eyes flashed with a guarded pleasure when she spoke of it.  He expected that such an item was not a common find.
   He extended his mind into the stone, he felt for the grooves where energy had been worked before.  Drawing forth the memory of the weaves, he opened his source and drew forth Alir from inside of him.  Chala snapped her fingers.  “Not from you, from around you, we do not have time to practice your multiple linking anymore.  We must practice it all at once.”  
   He gritted his teeth and felt out with a hundred tiny fingers.  He had figured out that it was simpler to find things like trees, or streams to draw potential energy from.  Chala drew it from the air, and grass.  Thousands of tiny weaves all at once.  He drew from many different pockets, he pointedly ignored his wyvern.  He’d tried that once, but she also saw that as cheating.  He did his best to duplicate the weave, and fill the same grooves in the stone that she had before.  It was difficult, and taxing to work with, but after twenty seconds he felt the stone grow warm under his fingers.  Then it began to glow with a dull light, not nearly as bright as it had been earlier, but bright nonetheless.
   His teacher made notes and corrections, but he could tell that she was generally pleased with the outcome of the lesson.  He had been improving fast according to her, and for her to even mention it meant so much more then what she said.  She had taught hundreds, if not thousands of students in her lifetime.  For her to be surprised at him, then he must’ve been a particularly quick learner.
   They travelled on, continuing their lessons as they got nearer, and nearer to Ford.  She began to teach him techniques on holding object aloft in the air.  They worked on nothing but that for almost two days until he could hold a ball of water three feet above the ground for a full minute.  Water was the most difficult thing to hold, other than air, because it is always changing and is so unpredictable when working a flow.
   He conquered every task she gave him, and he learned many different uses for the Alir.
   After another week they had arrived at Ford, though they hadn’t decided on a place to meet there were only a few inns in the town.  They went from tavern to tavern looking and asking around until they found a group of five people sitting at a booth far away from the rest of the crowd.
   Brazen walked towards it and heard a gasp.  All five of them turned.  Eric didn’t recognize two of them, but Ignis, Revlin, and Sarai had all made it here safely.  Even if they did look rather haphazard from the road.
   They pulled up another table and moved it close to theirs, and they all took their seats.  They looked from one to another, and Brazen raised an eyebrow at Sarai, motioning towards Droft and Hart as if to ask.  Can they be trusted?
   Sarai nodded, and an air of relaxation settled over them.  Chala had come with them, her form was covered while she was in public though.  Brazen ordered up drinks for all of them, and then began to talk with Hart and Droft.
   They talked about the war, and taxes.  Mostly small talk, but he could tell that Brazen was sizing them up throughout the whole of the conversation.  They ordered another round of drinks, and continued their conversations which began to grow more and more enthusiastic.
   “Wouldn’t ‘ta believed it ‘f I hadn’t seed it meself.  Blasted ground blowin’ up like a…”  Droft made a vague motion with his hands, and tried to find the right word.  It evaded him, so he continued.  “Bloody lost three men that day, ev’ry one o’ them bloody swordmaster in their own righ’.”  They all shook their heads.  Then Hart spoke to Brazen.
   “So Sarai tells me that you’re all part of the Du Vron Dezdant?  Some sort of special forces, is that true?  Lief raised an eyebrow at her and she shrugged.  
   “Yes I suppose you could say that, we have a very…wide range of abilities.”  Lief had just returned to the table from dancing with one of the barmaids, and his hair was still rather ruffled.  He sat down next to Droft, and took a heavy drink of mead.  He hiccupped, and then looked down at his tankard in shock.  “Holy shit this is good stuff, I’m already out.  Bartender!”  He raised his glass and signaled for one of the barmaids to come and refill his glass.  
   Hart waited until he was done, then continued.  “What kind of abilities do you have?  I must admit that I am rather curious of this division of the DuVron, of never heard of it.”  He lowered his voice and spoke quietly then.  “And if you were wondering I’m one of them too.  I’m all that’s left of Platoon VI, you know about that?”  He looked around as Droft and Ignis nodded.
   “Bloody suicide if yeh ask me.”  Ignis shook his head.
   “Stupid idea, prayed ev’ry nigh’ fer them men.”  Droft shook his head.
   Lief, only slightly drunk pried for answers.  “Wait, I never heard of ‘em.  What was Platoon Six?”  Droft took a swig from his ale.  Hart started to tear up and looked away, so Ignis sighed and started to tell him.
   Platoon VI was a force of close to one hundred men disguised as a merchant caravan.  They travelled to the heart of the Trojian capitol.  They were going to try to assassinate the entire Trojian court when they met for a rally.  Problem was they had underestimated the defenses around the castle, so when they thought they had been going up against thirty surprised guards, they came up against double that.  Alarms were raised and the reports received said that no one escaped.
   Lief raised his glass, “To Platoon Six, may every one of them rest in peace.”  
   “And take a million bloody Trojian’s wit’ em!”  Droft seconded, as they all emptied their glasses.
   They drank in peace for a few more moments, then rented rooms from the innkeeper and settled in for the night.  Even though there were three rooms, some of them were a little cramped.  Eric ended up on the floor next to one of the beds, and was so uncomfortable that he decided to go for a walk.
   He stayed as quiet as he could so that he didn’t wake any of the other occupants, and he started walking towards the woods.  He would go see his wyvern, he wanted to try out a few more names he had heard from Chala.
   After fifteen minutes he came to the clearing where he’d told it to hide.  He made a flash of energy above his head to signal for it, and not even ten seconds later it came bounding out of the brush.  It was energetic, like a puppy and when it nuzzled him its energy hummed low pitched and pleased-sounding.
   “I’ve got some more names to try out, you interested?”  It huffed, but settled down on the ground so that he could lay on it comfortably.  It grunted as if to ask, How many this time?  He held up two fingers and it shook its head, making it uncomfortable for a second until he readjusted himself.
   They laid like that for a moment, then Eric asked.  “Alright wanna hear ‘em?”  He didn’t wait for a response before giving it the first one.  “Alright here it is…this one is what Chala uses when she speaks of fate.  The name is AY’marra.  What do you think?”  
   The wyvern was thoughtful for a moment, then rumbled and shook its head.  No, that is not my name.  
   Perturbed, Eric tried again.  “Alright I’ve got one more.  I really liked this one.  It’s Solvyri, and it means ‘Child of the Sun.’” When it heard the name the wyvern grew warmer.  It nuzzled his neck and the energy it omitted was a deeper pitch.
   “Wait, Solvyri?  That’s your name.”  Again the wyvern thrummed with the energy.  The name seemed to invigorate it.  He laughed, “Alright then, Solvyri you have a name now.  So I don’t have to keep calling you ‘that thing,’ or ‘it’ anymore.”  Solvyri just kept thrumming with energy at the sound of the name, and they fell asleep right there underneath the stars.




































Chapter 36:  Like A Blade


   General Gundleas paced the dimly lit room that was the meeting hall for the leaders of the Du Vron Dezdant.  He was trying to think of how to tell them what news he had just received without driving them into a panic.  
   The Trojians had begun to push through the pass into Mercia, they had developed a new weapon that allowed them to eat away at the Mercian defenses bit by bit.  The Dezdant troops had found a choke point and were holding it with all that they had.  Even with that the amount of time left before they broke through could be measured in days.  When that happened their only hope would be in the Vervadian forces.  It might be possible for a messenger to get there in time for the Vervadians to arrive in time to flank them, but that was only a minuscule possibility.  Most likely they would flee the mountains and try to find a home in one of the villages near Thumbria, but he knew there was no real hope there.  If they lost Mercia then they were done, no second chances.  They had to get it right here.
   Gundleas made his preliminary statements to the council.  Of taxes, crime rates, and medical statuses.  Of the number of goods being used up by the people, and of the number they received in comparison.  He avoided the topic of the war front until he was asked directly.
   “What news do we have from the front Gundleas?  You have been avoiding the subject from the very start.  What ails you?”  The general sighed loudly.
   He walked over to the map of Mercia that was being displayed and motioned at the men marked onto it that showed where fighting was taking place.  “The pass is almost breached, we’ve no more than five days to have all of Mercia evacuated otherwise they will all be vulnerable should the Trojians find the Dezdant hideout.”
   They didn’t take it nearly as bad as he thought they would, a few of them shouted, a few wailed, but the majority of them simply shifted uncomfortably in their seats.  None of them said a word once the silence came upon them though.  Their eyes were all on him as he struggled to find the correct words to say.  They seemed to be trusting him with command over the situation, which for a group of mostly old nobles was a rather surprising thought.  
   His eyes swept over the men in front of him, then he coughed.  “Does anyone have any suggestions on what we could do to help prevent this crisis?”  Nobody said anything, they looked up at him from their seats and seemed to expect him to come up with a solution.
   “Alright here’s my plan.”  He motioned to the map in front of him.  “There are three points that I’m sure the enemy will have to cross on their way here.”  He stuck three pins into the map.  Each one marked a crossroads that any approaching army would have to move through.  “My plan is to catch them at these points by building fortifications in their way.  We will need everyone to help with this.  Once the first station is built then it will be fortified by a large contingent of soldiers.  There will be battle spikes, and as many different kinds of traps that our engineers can design.”
   He took a breath, this would be the hard part.  “I will also expect all people able to fight to do so.  Older boys, widows, and uncontested maidens will all fight if they can hold a sword.  They live here, they shall die here to defend it if the need is right.”  There was no uproar, they still wanted him to lead them in this fight, even though some of his tactics were less…tasteful.
   Gundleas stopped for a moment.  He looked at all the men in turn then asked them all.  “Who do you wish to lead the defenses of Mercia?  If anyone has any thoughts on potential candidates please speak quickly, as you now know we have very little time.”  
   One of the men raised his glass.  “Gundleas shall lead us, he is the most able.”  The rest followed suit, and raised their glasses in toast to Gundleas as he bowed deeply to them all in turn.
   “Is it in agreement then, shall I lead the defenses of the Du Vron Dezdant?”  Many of the men nodded.  They took a vote and not a man spoke otherwise against him.  Perhaps it was the fact that none of them had ever faced battle themselves that they had chosen him to lead.  Oftentimes the boldest or most courageous warrior takes the battle, or the most cunning.  People who had never stood among the battle lines, with your comrades dying beside you, thought of war as an ordered act.
   It was not ordered, it was not neat.  It was ugly and grisly, but necessary.  The general of such havoc did nothing more than inspire the people who were on the lines, and try to see the things that others might’ve missed.  A general could be a military genius, but if he didn’t inspire his troops to go above and beyond their call of duty then he could do no more than an ordinary man.
   Gundleas was one of those leaders of legend, he was a rock.  Nothing seemed to shake him, and he seemed to always have a way out planned, or another layer to the battle fabric.  He fought like a god, and would join his troops in battle.  In another time, fighting under a different flag, he would’ve been remembered as one of the greatest generals in history.
   But at this time, in this room, he felt weary.  The moment power had been granted to him his eyelids sagged, and he felt tired.  He was older then he’d been fifteen years ago, the fire that had filled him before had almost completely left, and the void inside him was as gaping and endless as the night sky. 
   “If you wish it I will lead, give the order for the troops to be mobilized.  Send runners to all the towns along the coast asking for aid.  Do not force any to join us, but take all that wish to.”  His eyes glinted steely in the dim light.  “Women, children, old and young.  We will not turn any away.”  Some of the men made confused faces, but none of them protested.  Gundleas bowed to them again and left the room without another word.
   Those fools thought they could win again, the memory of the last victory was still too fresh in their minds.   They thought themselves invincible.  He shook his head and continued down the corridor that led to his estates.  He walked up the steps and into the entrance hall.
   He sent a runner to the main barracks telling the officers to organize their assigned groups of soldiers for a briefing in the yard.  They would be ready by that night where he would give a speech, he thought about preparing one, but he had given so many that they came naturally to him by now.  He sent more messengers to everywhere in the city that prepared food, or had stores set away for times like this.
   The rest of his afternoon went mostly the same way, he would receive reports from his servants or official runners, and respond to the information received.  By that night most of the soldiers had gathered in the Yard to hear what the leaders and he had to say.
   There was no elevated platform, simply a mound in the center of the field that provided a natural spot for speeches to be given.  Around forty men had gathered atop of it, with the Dezdant leaders making up the majority of them.  The rest consisted of Gundleas, a small group of officers, and ten guards interspersed among them.
   After a few minutes of standing there one of the Seat raised their hand to call for silence.  The troops quieted down, and waited for them to begin.  The man who had raised his hand spoke in a clear, cool voice.  “We have called this assembly here today to address a certain number of matters on hand.  We have received word from the front that the Trojian forces have begun to break through our lines.”  There was muttering among the crowd, but most of them listened attentively.  “I present to you General Gundleas, who has been chosen to lead the Du Vron Dezdant’s forces in the upcoming confrontations.”  The man bowed and walked over to the empty space he had left with the rest of the Seat.
   Gundleas stepped forward, he was not nervous and each step he took seemed to turn his face more and more stone-like in appearance.  By the time he stood in front of the soldiers it was as if he was wearing a granite mask over his features.
   “The reports that have come from the front lines do not bode well for us.  They say that the Trojians have somewhere between thirty, and forty thousand troops pounding on our front door.  The most we can hope to muster is half of that.”  He gazed calmly out towards the crowd, the mask on his face seemed to crack a little.  Giving a glimpse of what was inside, a man who was always afraid for the things he loved and who would do anything to protect them.
   “I would not ask you to leave your families, they are what is most important.  I, however, believe that the best I can do for them is to prevent the Trojians from reaching their homes.  I will be walking to the pass at the border in two days time.  Any who wish to join me are more than welcome.  That is it.” 
   As he walked back to his spot by the Seat a few of them cast irritated glances at him.  They had surely expected him to launch into an hour-long speech that inspired every man within twenty miles to pick up his arms and fight.  What they did not know is that, if this war came down to numbers the Trojians would destroy them in an instant.  He could do more with a dedicated group of five-thousand men then he could with an unhappy twenty-thousand.  
   He was confident that an adequate amount of men would join him, how many did would determine his method of defense.  He would cross that bridge when he came to it.  For now he needed sleep, and to make love to his wife.  He ran a hand through his beard, yes he needed that.  She would worry, and so would he.  
   Extraordinary deeds would come soon enough, it was the simple things that held a man to the ground.
Chapter 37:  A Decision


   The six and Brazen sat around a campfire by the side of the road.  Hart, Droft, and Sa’shaset had turned in wordlessly.  As if they could feel that there were larger things going on behind the scenes.  Nobody spoke, they all seemed to be waiting for somebody else to start.
   Finally the silence was broken.  “So we’ve spoken about how we should come together.  We planned for that, but now we are all here.”  Lief’s eyebrows wrinkled, then he continued.  “We know that we plan to hunt down the Y’marr, that is plain, but how do we plan to do it?  Should we start in Frandia, then try to find passage to Fiorra?  Or should we start in Mercia, and circle up ‘round the coast?  We’ve got many options, and I admit I do not know which way would be the most appropriate.”
   Lief stopped as Brazen stood up, his frail legs trembled slightly from the cold.  “I believe that we should start in Mercia.  We may be able to convince the Du Vron Dezdant to sympathize with us, and they could be a very valuable asset to us later on in our journey.”  
   Ignis made a face, “I don’ know if it’d be real smart to try makin’ it inta there righ’ now.  There’s a standstill goin’ on righ’ now at the pass, and tha’s about the only way inta Mercia without goin’ all the way roun’ the Glandraung Mountains.”  He shrugged.  Obviously there was something deeper bothering him, but nobody inquired about it.  Instead Brazen shot down the statement.
   “There’s countless ways through those mountains, and many of them have worn into roads.  How else could Mercia have continued to trade and flourish even with the Trojian army on its doorstep?  I know of a way through those mountains, it’s a path that’s probably well worn in by now.  We could probably even get the wagon through.  And it leads right close to the headquarters of the Du Vron Dezdant.”
   Sarai nodded in agreement, as well as Eric and Lief.  Adelia wrinkled her eyebrows then shrugged.  “It seems like a good enough plan for me.”  Ignis started to fidget with his hands, but he nodded with the others after a moment.
   Brazen clapped his hands together with a sense of finality.  “Then it’s settled.  We’ll go south to Mercia through the Glandraung Mountains.  I’m sure the Du Vron Dezdant will want to ask me all about you six.  I’ve told them about the descendants of Mercolas, but they’ve never actually met one of them, let alone six.”  He paused for a moment and bit his lip.  “But I want you all to remember our goal, we can’t afford to be sidetracked by the Du Vron Dezdant, and they will surely try to enlist all of you.  You must stay weary, and not lose sight of the Y’marr, because that will be what matters most in the years to come.”
   They all agreed, and started small conversations for the next half-hour or so before, one-by-one, they began to settle into their respective cots.  
   Eric was the last one awake, he had sat watching the fire for nearly an hour.  Solvyri’s head was resting on his leg, and his thrumming energy was relaxing him.  He could always tell where the wyvern was, and what it was feeling.  It was a feeling that seemed to be growing more and more every day.  He was becoming more and more aware of its feelings, and its tendencies.  He could tell that it was aware of him in a similar way.  He was looking forward to the day when they could fight together.
   The idea struck him, why hadn’t he been training with Solvyri from the very first moment they’d come together in Thumbria?  The thought hadn’t even crossed his mind, he’d been so enthralled with the idea of becoming a Drunair that he’d neglected his training.
   He would have to make sure to work with Solvyri over their time together.  He would have to alternate between flying, and training with Chala.  
   He crawled down and settled into its chest.  It was always warm, as if there was a fire inside of him.  The warmth that seeped into him relaxed him, and the pitch changed deeper.  It was responding to his relaxation, and they both fell asleep together like that.  With the spot on Solvyri’s chest pulsing slightly in the night.










            


The Battle for Mercia


   “Just wait, until you see the enemy coming towards us with flags raised.  When you see the sky darkened with arrows, and the valleys run red with blood.  When you see what little remains of your lives torn away and destroyed without a second thought.  When you feel the cold fear grip your heart as you know, yes you know, that you are about to die.  When every breath seems a little more precious than the last, and you wish you could kiss your wife one last time before the bitter end, wish you could hold your son one last time, and watch your daughter grow into a woman.  You’ll know that all this is lost.  Everything has to come to an end, and you have no choice any longer.  Remember my words brothers, to the last man we shall fight.  When all this comes to pass remember us.  Do not forget why we gave our lives….this is all we ask.”  


- General Gundleas, White Rider of Mercia


Part One:  The Beginning of The End


   Gundleas walked along the ditch that was being dug at the first of the three positions that were being fortified.  It had been four days since he’d walked out of the headquarters of the Du Vron Dezdant with close to ten-thousand troops.  He was pleased with the number of men that had agreed to march with him, it was definitely enough.  Not so many that it would be difficult to maneuver, but not so little that they would be overrun.
   He had set every man to work at the first of the positions, the dense forests would protect their flanks on either side and prevent any cavalry from being effective.  Most likely the Trojians would try to run them down from the start, and that was where the first of his traps would fall into place.
   Hidden in caves all along the mountains that surrounded the road, one-third of his men would divide themselves out among multiple positions on the left and right flanks.  While the bulk of the enemy fought along the road these men would launch raids against the encampment.  They would aim for the supply wagons, and the horse stables.  They would torch as much as possible and then get out.  They would dress like marauders and run west, before doubling back to the mountains on their flanks.  Hopefully they would be able to launch two or three such attacks a day and cause the Trojians to take men from the lines in order to guard their supplies.
   Then, after two or three days of fighting, the enemy would be dug in.  They would begin mounting their large-scale weapons.  They would begin to grow impatient, and their attacks would grow more erratic as the men thought about the spoils ahead.  That was when they would run.  
   They would have enough food in their packs to ensure that they could make it to the next position, but other than their armor and weapons, they would leave everything behind.  Tents and supplies would all be left where they were.  Campfires would be left burning, and the men would set the forest on fire.  It was almost fall and the leaves and brush were drying out.  The blaze would catch quick, and should provide the men enough of a lead to make it to the next position.  They would not need to worry about provisions, all of that was already waiting at the second fortification, but the Trojians on the other hand would not have such a luxury.  They would take an entire day to pack everything away so that they could still maintain a siege at the Dezdant headquarters.
   He finished his inspection of the line, it was sloppy at best but there was still time before the Trojians arrived for them to finish it.  There were long pikes stuck in the ground in front of the ditch.  The ditch was eight feet across, and about four feet deep.  The bottom was covered in planks, and driven upside-down were four inch long nails.  They were spaced a hands length apart and covered the entire bottom of the ditch seamlessly.  It was the perfect type of defense for this situation, the ditch was just long enough that an armored man couldn’t jump it.
   Bags of sand had been positioned about thirty meters behind the ditch, there were plenty of spaces there for archers to fire upon the enemy while the line held them.  
   Another two-hundred meters back was a wooden palisade that had been erected.  It had been made quickly, but it was strong.  With the right tactics he could hold this ground for at least two days, hopefully more, while men worked on even more elaborate fortifications further east.
   It was one day later that a messenger came to him with news that the Trojians had finally broken through.
   It was two days after that when the battle began.




   “Sir, we’ve received word from our scouts that say the Trojians are mere miles away.  They’ll be here within two hours.”  Gundleas set his jaw and nodded to the man.  Then set about issuing orders to his officers.  The area where they would be fighting was very narrow, only one-hundred meters wide.  He should have very quick communication with his officers, and he would need that in the very near future.  The enemy would become congested very quickly, some might even try to work their way around the mountains to come around their flanks.  No, they wouldn’t do that.  They knew they could win, even on this ground.
   He had laid his cards out on the table, now he’d see how they played out.
   After fifteen minutes of relaying the plan to his officers again, Gundleas walked to the center of the line.  He drew his saxe and took a place on the first line.  His presence seemed to lift the spirits of the men next to him, just the fact that their general was fighting beside them was enough to inspire the men to greatness.  The enemy would be there very soon, and then they would see how this would end.
   He could feel the tension in the air like a taut bowstring, he glanced around at the men around him.  The men at the front had been chosen especially for their station, they were all strong and fit.  Most of them had hardened leather armor covering most of their bodies, a few of them were wealthy enough to afford chainmail, or had generous and wealthy friends that had given them the stuff.
   Gundleas’ armor consisted of a leather jerkin that sat underneath a chainmail shirt.  He had steel plated greaves, and hobnailed boots.  His helmet was steel and sat upon a chainmail cap, it was adorned with runes and sigils that wound their way around the entire helm like some philosopher’s crown.  It had a nosepiece that came down from the brow, and had two small wings on the crown of his head.
   Compared to the average soldier on the line he stood out, but not so much that it would be obvious that he was in command.  He would probably be viewed as some nobleman’s son and forgotten by the officers.  War was filled with such people, people who thought that victory was something achieved through standard tactics, and honorable duels.  This was something that Gundleas was counting on.  The Trojians had been very victorious in their earlier campaigns.  Some of their soldiers might have never known defeat.  There was a certain strength that this lent them, they would fight better and obey orders without hesitation.  But what they would lack was endurance.  Soldiers like that expected, and were used to, a quick victory.  Once the weariness set in, and they began to take casualties, the men would deflate quickly.  
   This was what his plan hinged on, he planned to draw them out and take away as much of their confidence as possible.  Make them hate every step they took deeper into Mercia so that, when they fought, they would fight like demons and forget their discipline.  They would break formation because of the bloodlust and become little more than beasts on the field.  He’d like to see the Trojian commanders try to keep a leash on that many men.
   He felt a tap on his shoulder, he turned and saw the captain of the two Blademaster regiments standing beside him.  Gundleas laughed aloud, “Hordrar, I was beginning to think you would never show.  How many blades have you brought with you?”  
   Hordrar was built very slim, he was not tall either.  He weighed at most one-hundred and seventy pounds, and came to Gundleas’ chin.  Nonetheless the man was the best swordsman in any of the kingdoms.  He had never been bested, and it was said he could fight for twenty hours without needing rest or food, only his Chromatis blade and the energy he had stored in it.  His arrival was an enormous relief to Gundleas, with Hordrar and seventy-five Blademasters he could hold this position for twenty-four hours at least.
